Received same day.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to Captain Bickley [at Swally], December</span> 24, 1630 (<em>O.C.</em> 1331).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The cotton yarn should not be shipped, pending the arrival of other goods intended for Persia. The latter should be put on board first, in order to ascertain how many bales of silk can be stowed in the two ships. Thereupon he will determine whether the cotton yarn should be taken or not. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Henry Sill, Christopher Read, and Nicholas Bix at Armagon to [the Bantam Factors], December</span> 27, 1630 (<em>O.C.</em> 1332).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The departure of the <em>Falcon</em> has been delayed by foul weather and the bad dealing of the Governors of Masulipatam and Petapoli. Send copies of their last letter, with the bill of lading, etc. Their <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-164" data-page="164" data-folio="118" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n163_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 118">[p. 118]</button> hopes of a better cargo than that sent by the <em>Dove</em> have been frustrated. The woven cloth of this place is particularly bad now. ‘Yet cannot wee justly blame our heere merchants, if wee conscider the dearenes of cotton, beeing fower times the price of former times and yeares, with the extraordinarie dearth and famine that hath predominated over all these partes, which hath consumed, meerely through want of sustinance, in each towne and villadge soe many of the people (and espetially the poorer sorte) as allmost incredible to reporte, were not ourselves spectatours. But to recount the many miseries in theis parts this yeare would better becom a historie then a letter of advise. And should the goodnes of the cloth have bin corrispondent to former times, our price must have followed; which wee verry well know the condicions of these people [i. e. the purchasers in the Far East] to bee such that they would not bee drawne thereto; whoe rather rule themselves by an accustomed price then the goodnes of the commoditie they buy; neyther can they bee sencible of its heere dearth and scarecetie when the Dutch and wee shall fill our there factories (which this yeare will bee) as if noe obstacles were heere to the contrarie. Heerewith wee send you coppies of our President and Councell at Suratt their letters to us heere, whereby you will understand that this plague of dearth and famine hath soe fully possest those parts as to make them doubt the loosing of a monsoone for England.’ The Company’s letters will apprise them of ‘the diverting of your there Presidencie to an Agencie, of which title this place is dispossest (as is but reason), with order heerafter to give an accompt to the President and Councell in Suratt.’ Send copy of their general letter to that place, and refer to the bearer, Lawrence Henley, for further information about affairs on this coast. Lading of the <em>Falcon</em>. Division of the Masulipatam charges between the two stocks. Request advice of how the several goods sent in the <em>Dove</em> and this ship find vent. Money due from a native merchant to be recovered and transmitted to Surat. Satisfaction to be given to the Dutch for a cable supplied to the <em>Falcon</em>. Passage granted to two Danish merchants, one of whom rendered them great service in a dispute at Petapoli; they desire to go to Macassar, to meet their General. Three Danish soldiers also given passages to Bantam. They had been enlisted ‘in the time of the Portugall <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-165" data-page="165" data-folio="119" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n164_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 119">[p. 119]</button> forces uppon this coast ’, but since dismissed, owing to the Company’s order that none shall be admitted into their service except with their approval; and owing to the famine they have petitioned to be taken out of the country. (<em>Copy. Received at Bantam February</em> 11, 1631. 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Rastell at Surat to John Bangham at Swally Marine, December</span> 29, 1630 (<em>O.C.</em> 1333).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The bales complained of for their magnitude belong to their best friends, the Shāhbandar, ‘ Meirzedalee [? Mīrzā Alī], Salle Mahmud [Sālih Mahmūd], Agigialla [Azīzullāh], the Governours brother, etc.,’ and must be laden on board some ship even if part of the Company’s cotton yarn be left behind, ‘ the faire passage and nimble dispatch of our business this yeare in generall having deserved farr greater courtesie.’ Preference in lading to be given to certain persons. <em>PS.</em>—Thirteen more fardles belonging to ‘ Agigialla ’ to be shipped. <em>PPS.</em>—Certain other goods to be laden. (<em>Seal, with the Company’s arms.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Rastell and Messrs. Hopkinson, Bickford, and Suffield at Surat to [the Commanders of the Fleet from England]</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-090" href="#sec-04-fn-090" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-090" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, <span class="sc">December</span> 31, 1630 (<em>O.C.</em> 1334).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Are aware of the Company’s orders limiting the stay of the fleet at the Comoros, in expectation of the <em>William</em> and <em>Blessing</em>, to July 10 next. These ships would have been sent direct to meet them, but it has been found necessary to dispatch them first to Bantam, in company with the <em>James</em>, for her better defence against the Portuguese. Have therefore sent the present ‘ advizor ’ to acquaint them of this and to require them to wait at the Comoros until August 20, by which time the Bantam fleet has orders to join them there. In the event of the latter failing to appear by that date, they must proceed alone to Persia and land their cargoes designed for that country. They should wait there for the Bantam fleet until October 20, and then come on to Surat, without meddling with any of the estate of the First or Second Voyage in Persia. They are entreated to be very wary of the ‘ stratagems by fire plots ’ of the Portuguese; ‘ and having to deale with frigotts, to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-166" data-page="166" data-folio="120" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n165_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 120">[p. 120]</button> bee well furnished with stones in your shipps topps and other places, with halfe tubbs of water in all parts of your shipps, and with firepotts to answere them in their owne element, as in your better judgments shall seeme convenient. Your people also of all degrees to be daily disciplined and exercised to their smale shott, which must now serve you to most principall use, as by this yeares service wee have with good successe experienced. And lastly, it would behoove you during your abode about theis [Comoro] Ilands to be alwayes vigilant, with your shipps priddy <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-091" href="#sec-04-fn-091" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-091" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and ordenance cleere upon all occasions in expectance of the enemyes soddaine encounter there; where it is supposed they intend some exploite, if not this, yett the next yeare, upon our outward bound shipps, and wee wonder they have thus longe neglected so faire an opportunity.’ (<em>Copy. Received, June</em> 20, 1634,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-092" href="#sec-04-fn-092" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-092" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> <em>from Plymouth out of Capt. Quail’s ship.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Rastell and Messrs. Hopkinson, Bickford, and Suffield at Surat to the Company, December</span> 31, 1630 (<em>O.C.</em> 1335).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-093" href="#sec-04-fn-093" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-093" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Send a copy of the last letter from this place, dated April 12, 1630; also of one written by the President from the Bay of St. Augustine on July 26. He left that place on July 27, with the <em>James</em>, <em>William</em>, and <em>Blessing</em>, and proceeded to Johanna, where he met the <em>Discovery</em> and <em>Reformation</em>. Finding that time would not permit of their going first to Persia, all five ships came straight to Surat, arriving on September 22. Trouble with the Portuguese frigates. ‘Forasmuch as these frigats, not many dayes before, had made seazure of the <em>Musshawhee</em> (a junck of this place), returned rich with treasure, etc., from Mocha, and for that these marchants were in dayly expectacion of their greater shipp <em>Shawhee</em>, with a wealth by farr surpassing the other, the feare of whose miscariage also did not a little perplex them, wee, uppon their generall petitionary importunitys and to endeare them the more to our freindship (especially upon the President, Mr. Rastell, his first establishment), were moved to send out the whole fleet againe in quest of the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-167" data-page="167" data-folio="121" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n166_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 121">[p. 121]</button> <em>Shawhee</em>; who off of Damon, being followed by the frigats, had occasion given to spoile two or three of them with their great ordnance, and therewithall slew some of their people, whose certaine nomber they have concealed. The 14th of October following our fleet againe returned; and being gotten into the Hole of Swally, were soone visited againe by the frigats; who, in the intrim of our discharging treasure, landed divers of their souldiers, which ours as suddainly confronted; but as then not any thing attempted. Neverthelesse, the next day ensuing, being Saboth (and the President, etc., yet aboard), their Captain Maior, with the Vice-Kings sonne and about 160 souldiers well armed, comes the second tyme on shoare, and now somewhat neerer our tents then before; when upon the instant (not having tyme to consult) wee enordered a like nomber of our choycest musketteirs, and the comaunders (thereto importunatly willing of themselves) to be their leaders for their better incouragment in the encounter; who, deviding their number into three squadrons, deceived the enemy with the one of them only in sight, whilst the other two were marcht about the sandhilles, and afterwards rejoyning all into one body againe at an apointed distance, with very good discipline were (by the enemyes retreate to the water side) now engaged within pistoll shott of their frigats, the Portingall haveing purposly so contrived it, in strong confidence of their owne shelter and the galling of our English with their ordnance. But ours very providently perceaving that but three of their prowes could offend them, and those also so ill plied as, for feare of their owne, they could not well damnifie our people, they feircly entred amongst them and in the very face of their vessells pursued them into the water chin deep; where with admirable resolucion they so prosecuted their fury that even to the frigats sides they continued the slaughter; and haveing massacred the greater part, whereof many by small shot and others at handy blowes with their swords and musket stocks, returned with a glorious victory and 27 Portingalls brought off alive their prisoners. This they happily performed (without other harme then [<em>blank</em>] men wounded and one man suffocated only with heate) in the sight of divers Mogulls and other these country people, who, in admiration of so strange a manner of fight, have disperced their letters both to the court and severall parts of this kingdome, and were pleased <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-168" data-page="168" data-folio="122" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n167_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 122">[p. 122]</button> to aver the like battaile to have never bin seene, heard of, or ever read off in storyes; which wee have embouldened to insist on the more, by how much it hath added more to our nations fame then hath all our sea fights formerly acquired here in India. And, as for this greate blessing, so must wee yet add our further acknowledgment to the same God, by whose good providence also your ships within a few dayes after were wonderfully delivered from their maine plotted stratagem by fire; which at a darke moone, observing their advantage of winde and tide, they put in execution the 24th at night; having for that purpose prepared four vessells stuft up with combustable matter, which, chained two and two together and brought on with their frigats within a reasonable distance, they fired and let drive amongst our fleet. But our people being nightly vigilant and ever ready prepared, with their boats well mand ahead of the ships, very bouldly with their grapnells fastened on the fier boats, which being towed, two of them on the shoare and other two on the sands, there fruitlessly spent themselves, to the enemyes great appall and our peoples greater joy, who for many nights before had even tired out themselves with watching to accomplish this great prevention. These were the disturbances which your President, etc., were to struggle with at their first arrivall. And not these alone, but others also, though not so daungerous, yet difficult too, by reason of an universall dearth over all this continent, of whose like in these parts noe former age hath record; the country being wholy dismanteled by drougth, and to those that were not formerly provided noe graine for either man or beast to be purchast for money, though at seavenfould the price of former tymes acustomed; the poore mechaniques, weavers, washers, dyers, etc., abandoning their habitacions in multitudes, and instead of releife elcewhere have perished in the feilds for want of food to sustaine them. Hence it came to pass that for many dayes after our arrivall there were noe carts or beasts of burden to be had upon any condition whatsoever; by which meanes for a while wee were greatly hindred in the usuall prosecution of our bussines, till from the inland countrye (where was some better plenty for cattell) wee were otherwise provided.' In this interim they allotted to the factors their respective employments, framed their instructions, and supplied them with the necessary means; besides sending orders and advices <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-169" data-page="169" data-folio="123" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n168_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 123">[p. 123]</button> to the Coast and to the southwards. Now proceed to answer such points in the Company's letter of March 9, 1630, as are pertinent to them. Are fully sensible of the hazard caused by the late dispatch of the ships from India, and to prevent it this year have arranged that the fleet for England shall go straight home from Persia. This will prevent their losing the monsoon; and although it will expose the three vessels which are to return to this port to great risk from the Portuguese (the Viceroy being daily expected with 120 frigates, five galleys, and some fresh fireboats, and there being no news of the Dutch fleet from Batavia), yet it is the lesser evil of the two. Have used all possible endeavours to prevent the cutting open of the bales of calico, either by the natives or by the English. Thought it best to land their small stock of gold here rather than send it to Persia. It is now somewhat fallen in price, 'by reason of this extraordinary dearth before touched, the richer sort falling short of their wonted incomes and profitts, and are therefore disabled of the meanes to buy and hourd up gould as in former tymes; and contrarywise the poorer people constrained to sell their goulden jewells to buy them food; whichfilter neverthelesse may not discourage your future supplyes in that specie, which will allwayes render you more profitt then ryalls, though therein you may please not to exceed above one-third gould and two-thirds ryalls, as being the fittingest proporcion in both as will best accomodate our occacions. The power which you are pleased to confer upon us, the better to restraine your sea commanders in their profuse expence of fresh victualls and extraordinary provizions in your ships, shalbe used with such discretion and waryness that, as we shall strive to conforme them to such fitting regularity therein as may owne the name of good husbandry, soe shall wee confine ourselves to a moderate lymit in the due execution of what may be urgently requisite in case of inconformity. Mr. Wildes proposition of setling a residency in Sinda hath bin a dispute of long antiquity, and therein such difficultyes doe present themselves as it stands not with your safety to wade to farr in that bussines, were it for noe other reason then the unaccessableness of your shipps to comand that shoare; which indeed was the mainest obstacle why there was not an established factory in that place long heretofore. And to transport any goods from thence by land to Surat hath in it soe much danger <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-170" data-page="170" data-folio="124" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n169_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 124">[p. 124]</button> and incertaynty, besides an excessive charge, that will render it altogether unprofitable.' Refrain from expressing an opinion regarding Boothby's case, as the Company have the means of forming their own judgements upon it. Note the orders regarding the Surat Council. Part of the money assigned to Persia has been invested in sugar, dry ginger, coffee, cinnamon, etc., 'for an entrance into that trade, so benefitiall unto others, and hetherto so much neglected for your better behoufes, which for the future we intend to goe still augmenting as wee finde incouragment thereunto.' Some of the goods assigned to Persia for presents are detained by the Governor of Surat. 'Wee have acquainted your servants here in the Mogulls country with the authorytye which you have been pleased to confer on Mr. Thomas Rastell, your President, for the generall direction and ordering of all your affaires in India; and have signified the like to our freinds at Musilpatam, as also your wills concerning the suppression of the Presidencie in Bantam and the frustrating of the power of judicature wherewith that President and Councell was formerly invested.' As regards the provision of goods for England, they have ordered 20,000 or 30,000 additional pieces of white calico, as the scarcity of Sarkhej indigo necessitates a reduction under that head. Have instructed the factors to buy no cloth that is deficient in length or breadth, and to pay attention to 'the closse making and foulding up of their broad and narrow baftaes', and also to their packing in proper fashion. Have ordered some Ahmadābād 'duttyes and narrow baftaes... but with caution that by altering of the weavers loomes they procure the long baftaes to be of equall breadths with those of Baroch, and their duttyes of full yard broad and 12 yeards long.' 'With symianoes or surreas, whether broad or narrowe, nether yet with capporees, populees, hassanees, or chuckarees, wee intend not to meddle untill your further order to the contrary; but of broad nickanees, thred tapceele, keeses blew and white, and those others also of the finer sort, you may expect to be furnished, both for quantity and quallity, agreeing with your owne direccions. And for callicoe lawnes or shashes, namely, sallowes, guldares, sursalles, and cuscosees, etc., wee will desist in the buying of any more untill you may be better confirmed in the sale of those already sent home; which, if transferd for Turkey, wee are confident would yeeld good <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-171" data-page="171" data-folio="125" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n170_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 125">[p. 125]</button> proffit and vend in infinite quantities ; for evidence whereof you may please to observe that they are the cheifest comodities wherein the Persians and Armenians who takes yearely passage on your shipps doe invest great summes of mony here in India ; some of them makeing instant sale thereof att Gumbroone, from whence they are dispersed and sould againe to second marchants in Spahan, Balzar [Basrā], Bagdat, etc., who transport them yet further for a third markett at Constantynople and other places, at extraordinary charge of camellhier, customes, and other exactions on the way ; and yet every of these, at the first, second, and third hand, doe become great gainers and make it their constant trade of liveing. Now, the premises considered, wee see noe reason why their should not be a divertion of this great traffique (thus expensive by land) by your less chargeable conveyance by sea (at least for a good partt thereof), as hath ben brought to passe allready for those great quantityes of indicoe and callicoes wherewith you now dayly furnish those forraigne parts yourselves, which in the selfe same kinde by land hath been formerly performed by others. Your commission injoyning a large investment this yeare in both sorts of indicoe, that wee might the better make choyce of the fewer quantities of callicoes required, is greatly crost by the great want of raines in these partts, especially about Amadavad, where their this yeares whole cropp on the ground is not likely to produce above two or three hundred fardles, which in former tymes hath not been soe little as 4 or 5,000. And for that of the passed yeares growth, the many buyers, as well Dutch as Persians, Armenians, etc., having furnished themselves with the choycest ware at excessive high rates, there is left but a poore remaines of refuse stuff behinde, enough only for this countryes service, and yet that not to be purchast under 18 rupes the maund ; which therefore wee determine not to meddle with, though at farr lesse rates, in respect of its badd condition.’ In lieu of Sarkhej they will send an increased quantity of Biāna indigo. If they buy any gum-lac they will be careful as to the quality ; but they think it can be got better at Masulipatam. In each ship they will send a quantity of doubly refined saltpetre to serve as ballast ; will put as much as possible into casks, but cannot thus pack the whole. Cotton yarn shall be provided as far as space is available in the absence of more valuable <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-172" data-page="172" data-folio="126" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n171_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 126">[p. 126]</button> goods ; at present, owing to the dearth, both that and cotton wool are at double their usual prices. Will send no spikenard, sugar, dry ginger, aloes Socotrina, quilts, or carpets until further orders. As regards the long pepper bought here for the Company at 27 mahmūdīs per maund, while the mariners procured some at 10 mahmūdīs, they explain that the latter was of a meaner sort, obtained at Bantam, ‘which this here doth farr transcende in goodness.’ Note the orders concerning green ginger and bloodstones. ‘Cinamon being a bulkey comoditie, and deare withall, is more fitt for Persia, but not profitable hence for England.’ As the <em>James</em> is to call at Surat on her way back from Bantam it will be cheaper to procure a lading of pepper from that place ; and on her arrival part of her cargo can be shot loose into the holds of the other ships (to fill up vacant spaces), and the room thus left can be utilized for richer goods. Have ordered the factors, in packing their goods, to use the best cotton wool and dutties. Have forbidden the shipment of oil, to avoid damage by leakage. Note the instructions regarding the 1630 and 1631 fleets. Most of the factors called home for private trading, etc., have already gone on the <em>Charles</em> and <em>Jonas</em>. Have been obliged to detain Skibbow (who is one of those named) because his accounts are not yet completed. They have found him both temperate and discreet ; and although he is not one of the three selected for the Council, yet in consideration of his position and long service he is permitted to take part in their consultations. Barber is returning in these ships (if he can finish his accounts in time) ; and as Richard Predys and William Price died before the arrival of the fleet, they are so shorthanded that they are obliged to detain Thomas Wilbraham and Richard Belfield to assist them. One, if not two factors, must necessarily follow the court, ‘for the opposing of complaints and the solliciting of other occasions’ ; but when the King returns to Agra this expense can be saved, as the factors resident there can undertake the duty. Persia and the southern factories are still worse off as regards assistants. In the case of the former, captains’ boys and other young men have been taken on shore for this purpose ; but they shall now be replaced by pursers or pursers’ mates. It will be more difficult to supply the southern factories ; at present they are waiting to hear from Sill and his colleagues on the subject. The goods <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-173" data-page="173" data-folio="127" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n172_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 127">[p. 127]</button> already laden for Persia on the Company’s account amount to 56,234 mahmūdīs. A further consignment, which has cost about 98,826 mahmūdīs, is on its way from Ahmadābād and Cambay, but they fear the ships will not be able to wait for it. Would gladly have enlarged the investment, ‘did not both warr and famine even frustrate all hopes in the vend of our English comodities, whose provenu [produce] wilbe a great want unto us for the accomplishing of other investments.’ Cannot tell who were the owners of the silk mentioned by Burt. The sword-blades sent for a trial are still detained in the customhouse; so it is impossible to say anything regarding their saleability. In case of transfer of stock, etc., from one Voyage to another, they will follow the directions given. Have laden on the <em>Discovery</em> and <em>Reformation</em> for England 456 fardles of Agra indigo, 579 bales of calico, 250 bales of cotton yarn, 597 bales of saltpetre (in lieu of ballast), and 4,509 maunds of Priaman pepper. These, with the 1,400 bales of silk expected to be shipped in Persia, will nearly equal the whole estate of the First Persia Voyage. The goods provided in India (as also 800 bales of Sarkhej indigo, to be sent home next year) have been bought partly with the money of the Second General Voyage; the cargoes must therefore be considered as sent on joint account, and the proceeds will have to be divided between the two Voyages. Cannot at present advise the exact proportion, but it will probably be two-thirds for the First Voyage and one-third for the Second. The Sarkhej indigo is kept back, partly to accelerate the dispatch of the ships, but chiefly to enhance its price at home, to compensate for the dear rates at which it has been bought here, viz. 16¼ rupees per maund. The calicoes, on the other hand, are cheaper than usual. The accounts now sent will show that they stand indebted (for the Old Stock) nearly 41,000<em>l.</em>, ‘besides its dayly growth by the addition of intrest uppon intrest att 1⅛ per month.’ Trust that the Company will take steps to discharge this liability, which is not only wasteful in itself but obliges them to conciliate their creditors to the prejudice of their business. At Bantam, on the other hand, the factors aver that they could lade with spices four of the Company’s best ships, but have no means of transporting this wealth. Have written to Willoughby to lade in the <em>James</em> on her arrival at Bantam a consignment of pepper, etc., of the Old <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-174" data-page="174" data-folio="128" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n173_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 128">[p. 128]</button> Stock, to be used towards the discharge of the debts here; also, if necessary, to borrow a further quantity of the same pepper for the Second General Voyage, replacing it by the time any shipping arrives from England to receive the remains of the Old Stock. This arrangement will be beneficial to both stocks. Have also suggested to Willoughby that he should fill a small vessel with sugar (which can be bought at Bantam at half the price demanded in Surat) for Persia, returning by way of this place next autumn. In future any ship intended for the Coast of Coromandel should leave England not later than August. The <em>Star</em> was much hindered by her late arrival. Refer to Willoughby's letters for information as to her proceedings on the Coast. Are not yet satisfied that two residences are necessary there; ‘though wee should be glad they might obtayne such immunities in Mesulapatam which the Dutch (if wee mistake not) did acquist by constraint and not by quiett treaty, yet wee shalbe very cautious how wee enter upon any great disbursements in fortifying att Armagon, unless there bee aparance of such hopes and benefitts thereby as may countervaile the charge and beare out that yearely expence of garrison proposed.’ Have ordered an inquiry into the private trade of Sill and other factors. Think Willoughby mistaken in charging him with incapacity, as he seems from his letters to be a judicious and well-experienced merchant. Disapprove Willoughby's action in hiring a separate house at Masulipatam. Received letters of July 31 and August 5 from Hoare at Bantam, stating that to oblige ‘certaine importunate Moores’ he had charged Surat with 8,377 rials 30<em>d.</em> on account of the Joint Stock, and giving information as to the state of the shipping in those parts. Number of vessels required there in future. Losses sustained by the Dutch at Jambi, etc. Think that the action of the latter in not sending a fleet from Batavia to Surat this year ‘savours rather of treachery then want of shipping’, as they were well aware of the preparations of the Portuguese. The Company will perceive how necessary it is to depend on their own power and not to scant shipping for these parts. In view of the danger from the Portuguese, have decided to send the <em>James</em>, <em>William</em>, and <em>Blessing</em> together to Bantam, thence to proceed to St. Augustine's Bay and the Comoros, to meet the new fleet from England. As it will <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-175" data-page="175" data-folio="129" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n174_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 129">[p. 129]</button> be necessary to warn the latter to wait longer there than the date fixed by the Company, they have bought and fitted out a frigate of about 40 tons to carry advices to those islands. Another letter to the same effect will be sent by a native vessel. Cloves procurable at Macassar. The intended dispatch of the <em>Swallow</em> from Bantam to England may frustrate the President’s plans for utilizing the pepper, etc., there to pay the Old Stock’s debts at Surat; it will therefore be advisable for the Company to send money to clear that liability. Sales made from the goods received by this year’s fleet. The cloth is the worst they have ever seen in India; it would be better to send none at all than such as this. The mariners bring a better quality and obtain 25 or 30 per cent. more in price. In future, 100 cloths will suffice, 80 of them stammel (no reds), and 20 green. It would be well to send for a trial ‘20 light coloured Spanish cloaths now usually worne in England by the gentry’, as ‘these great ones’ have often importuned for a supply. Of lead, 1,000 pigs will be a suitable quantity; the price at present is about seven mahmūdīs per maund. Six or seven hundred maunds of quicksilver may be supplied yearly. The seamen by their private sales brought down the price to 3½ mahmūdīs per seer; but steps have been taken to stop this by seizing the parcels thus sold in the customhouse, paying only the sale price for them. The quantities of rough amber and vermilion sent this year may be continued. Coral should be forborne one year, except for 20 or 30 chests of that of highest price, known as ‘Grezzi di Sardigna’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-094" href="#sec-04-fn-094" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-094" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> ‘This direfull tyme of dearth and the Kings continued warrs with the Decans disjoynted all trade out of frame; the former calamitie haveing fild the waies with desperate multitudes, who, setting their lives att nought, care not what they enterprize soe they may but purchase meanes for feeding, and will not dispence with the nakedest passenger, not soe much as our poore pattamars with letters, who, if not murthered on the way, doe seldome escape unryfled, and thereby our advises often miscarried on the other side. The warrs with Decan haveing stopped up all passages, the usuall intercourse of trade, the ordinary travell of caphilas, and accustomed conflewence of marchants to and from those parts are intercepted; <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-176" data-page="176" data-folio="130" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n175_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 130">[p. 130]</button> whereby the vend, not only of your currall (whose greatest expence is in Decan) wilbe hindered, but likewise your fraight and customes in Persia much lessened by the want of those finer goods out of Decan, in whose liew your ships are only fraught with these of groser quallitie.’ For this and other reasons, the freight earned by the present fleet for Persia amounts only to 22,000 mahmūdīs ; but the customs at Gombroon will be increased ‘ by meanes of five laden juncks of this place intended also for Persia, though their dispeed be somewhat retarded, in expectacion of Portingall passes promised upon their late reconcswiliacion made with this Kinge; who, highly inraged at their insolencyes, had by confederacy with Idle Shaw,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-095" href="#sec-04-fn-095" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-095" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> a neighbouring prince on the Portingalls territories, abridged them of all dayly provisions, and would have pursewed them to more streighter exegencie, as well there as other parts, to castigate their pride ; which they perceaveing, and about the same instant haveing received a fatall overthrow uppon the iland Seilon, by the losse of 700 Portingalls and all their houlds except Columba [Colombo] and one more, were humbled so low as, contrary to their accustomed haughtines and all former example of that kinde, to surrender up the surprized <em>Mushawhee</em> againe, with all her goods and treasure. That loss of theirs upon Seilon, together with their want of supplies out of Europe, being the two mean [main] happie obstacles which the Almighties providence had apointed to divert their intended power of 12 gallions prepareing in Goa, with halfe as many more expected from Lisbon, besides three gallies and 120 friggats, the most parte fitted to wreake their mallice uppon your single force. And yet, notwithstanding the premisies, being now lately exasperated by their notorious disgrace uppon Swally Sands, the Vice-Kinge, accompanied with Ruifrero, in the hope of the Hollanders not arrivall, is said to be now addressing hitherwards with four gallions, three gallies, and 80 frigats, with intent (as wee suppose) to awaite our remaining shipps in their backe returne. But soe may it come to passe that the sly Hollander may jussell them in their way, to prevent our further disturbance.’ Have instructed the commanders to keep together and to be always on their guard against surprise. Praise their ‘passed deportments’, which prove them <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-177" data-page="177" data-folio="131" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n176_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 131">[p. 131]</button> to be ‘tractable, carefull, and valliant commanders’. They are ordered not to remain at Gombroon later than March 1; and if the silk has not come down by that time (‘by reason of the rebellion in Guilon’ [Ghilān]), they are then to dispatch the <em>Discovery</em> to England alone, the <em>Reformation</em> returning to Surat with the other ships. The portion of Sir Francis Crane’s tapestry sent to court was sold to a nobleman named ‘Raw Rutton’ [<em>see</em> p. 90] but, as he is ‘one of those great leaders who are now in feild against the enemy’, there is no hope of payment until his return. Part of the tapestry retained here has been sold at a lower price to the Governor; but five pieces are still on hand. Are now treating with the Governor for the sale of the tapestry belonging to Captain Andrews and Mr. Hugh Perry. Send a list of the factors, etc., and their several employments in the Mogul’s country. Thomas Smith, who was met at St. Lawrence on his way back to England, was persuaded by the President to return to India. Others, whose period of service had nearly expired, were prevailed upon to remain three years longer at an increased salary; the most notable of these are Arthur Suffield, ‘one of our Councell,’ John Norris, chief in Broach, and Ralph Rand. Transmit their accounts, balanced to April 20, 1630. Barber has been unable to complete them to date, especially as the Agra accounts are not yet received, and those of Persia are two years behind. This has prevented his return to England; but his services in Cambay for the new investment will be very useful. The factors at Agra write that they have bought upwards of 400 bales of round indigo, but ‘in regard of the often itterated drougth and many greedy buyers’, the price has risen to 38 rupees per maund, and not much is to be had at that rate. Do not expect, therefore, more than five or six hundred fardles for next year’s fleet; and are consequently obliged to make a larger investment in calicoes. Could get no Malabar pepper, owing to the Deccan wars, and so were forced to buy that of Priaman at higher rates than usual. <em>PS.</em> (<em>January</em> 6, 1631)—Of the Portuguese prisoners, ten of the chief have been sent to England by these ships, and the rest will follow in the next fleet, as they consider it wise to deprive the enemy of ‘his naturall Portugalls here in India’. Beg for a supply of presents. Too many have been given of late; but this abuse can only be reformed <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-178" data-page="178" data-folio="132" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n177_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 132">[p. 132]</button> gradually. Request that the supply of elephants' teeth may be resumed, as the best are now worth 55 mahmūdīs per maund. Estate of William Price. Want of ink, quills and papers. Have now found that the sailors are the guilty parties in the matter of ripping up the bales of calico. Those discovered have been punished severely. Account of stores transferred from ship to ship. Charles Scott, a sailor, sent home in the <em>Discovery</em>. In the fight with the Portuguese he was shot through both legs; commend him to the Company's charity. (<em>Received April</em> 11, 1632, <em>by way of Persia and Turkey.</em> 24 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Wilbraham's Account of Cash Received and Spent at Swally, June–December</span>, 1630 (<em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. xxiv. p. 53<em>a</em>).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The payments are for cartage, the supply of water, imprest money, charges of merchandize, etc. The total is 18,040 mahmūdīs 12 pice. (1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Armagon Factory Ledger</span>, 1630–31 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 41).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The entries are dated between May 30, 1630, and January 22, 1631. The following names occur: Ambrose Winter, William Smith, Leonard Collinson, Richard Stampford, William Cliffe, Ethelbred Bright, John Butler, Jacob Fuddle, Lancelot Wigsell, Nicholas Bird, John Collins, Nicholas Bix, Richard Langham, Thomas Helliard, Richard Barlow, John Glover, Brian Barnes, Henry Cuffe, Thomas Pack, Francis Holland, Osmond Smith, Timothy Humbleton, John Turner, Griffith Price, Lawrence Henley, William Cross, Henry Sill, Ralph Cartwright, Edward Hall, Edward Chiverton. (23 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Vian's Account of the Voyage of the</span> <em>Discovery</em> <span class="sc">from Surat to Gombroon and thence to England</span> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. lii. p. 53).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1630, <em>December</em> 31. The ship was fully laden with goods for England and Persia; the latter were discharged at Gombroon, and 500 bales of silk taken on board. 1631, <em>January</em> 7. The whole fleet went out of Swally Hole. <em>January</em> 8. Sailed for Persia.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-179"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="179" data-folio="133" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories04fost/page/n178_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 133">[p. 133]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>January</em> 28. Saw the coast of Arabia. <em>February</em> 3 [5?]. Captain Green, master of the <em>Blessing</em>, died. <em>February</em> 7. Anchored at Gombroon. <em>March</em> 18. The fleet sailed, the <em>Discovery</em> and <em>Reformation</em> being bound for England, the pinnace [the <em>Intelligence</em>] for the Comoros, and the rest for Surat. <em>March</em> 24. The ships for Surat parted company near Jask. <em>March</em> 31. Met a vessell of ‘Synda’ bound for Gombroon. <em>April</em> 15. Saw the coast of India. <em>April</em> 18. Took some coco-nuts and ‘jagra’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-096" href="#sec-04-fn-096" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-096" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> from a Malabar junk. <em>April</em> 19. Lost sight of the coast near Cape ‘Comorine’. <em>April</em> 21. The pinnace departed for the Comoros to meet the fleet from England. <em>May</em> 6. A total eclipse of the moon. <em>May</em> 18. Made the island of ‘Deagoe Rise’ [Rodriguez]. <em>May</em> 20. Anchored at the NNE. end of Mauritius. <em>May</em> 21. Anchored to the west-wards of Turtle Bay. Found that the pinnace <em>Intelligence</em>, commanded by Mr. Burley, had reached the island on May 17 and departed on the 20th. <em>June</em> 1. Sailed for the Cape. <em>June</em> 3. Saw the island ‘John de Castro’ [<em>cf.</em> p. 42]. <em>June</em> 27. Lost company with the <em>Reformation</em> in a storm. <em>July</em> 5. Sighted the African coast. <em>July</em> 12. Met with the <em>Reformation</em> again. <em>July</em> 13. Anchored in Table Bay, finding there the <em>Palsgrave</em> and <em>London</em>, both bound for Bantam, and the <em>Holland</em>, a Dutch ship bound for Batavia. <em>August</em> 2. The four English ships sailed. <em>August</em> 19. The <em>Discovery</em> and <em>Reformation</em> anchored at St. Helena. Found there two Portuguese and a black, who had been left by a carrack homeward bound from Goa, that arrived at the island on July 20 and departed six days later. <em>August</em> 29. Sailed. <em>September</em> 5. Saw Ascension. <em>October</em> 26. Passed Flores. <em>October</em> 30. Another total eclipse of the moon. <em>November</em> 9. Sighted the Lizard. <em>November</em> 11. Spoke a small French ship from St. Malo, and heard of the conclusion of peace.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-097" href="#sec-04-fn-097" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-097" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> <em>November</em> 13. Off the South Foreland. Captain Bickley was carried ashore to be buried at Dover. Anchored in the Downs. <em>November</em> 16. Sailed again. <em>November</em> 28. Reached Erith. <em>December</em> 16. The cargo was fully discharged. (32 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-04-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-001" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For other accounts of this incident see the previous volume; also <em>infra</em>, p. 29.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Khān of Shīrāz.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Turban-cloths. For the other piece-goods see pp. 62, 63 of the previous volume.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-004" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-004" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Van Twist (<em>General Beschrijvinge van Indien</em>, 1648) explains that ‘ornijs’ are silken veils, ornamented with gold, used by women; and Pelsart (1627) has a note to the same effect. It is the Hind. <em>orhnī</em>, a general term for a woman's sheet or mantle.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-005" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-005" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">An error for mahmūdīs; see pp. 325, 329 of the previous volume.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-006" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-006" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This name was often applied to Lārībandar (the ‘Diul-Sind’ of the Portuguese), the port of Tatta, in Sind; but here Tatta itself is meant.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-007" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-007" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">An important step in the evolution of the Council.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-008" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-008" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Evidently each rial or dollar was taken at 5<em>s.</em></span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-009" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-009" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See a note on p. 65 of the previous volume.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-010" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-010" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-011" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘Wale’ is an old English word for a ridge of threads in a cloth. ‘Dimity’ and ‘diaper’ are well-known terms.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-012" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-012" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In <em>Factory Records: Celebes</em>, vol. i, will be found a copy of a further postscript, dated March 11, 1630, dealing with affairs at Macassar.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-013" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-013" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Compare the letter from Mīrzā Mahmūd printed at p. 325 of the previous volume.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-014" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-014" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Wrongly dated 1629. In addition to Wylde, Skibbow, and Page, the following signed this paper: Barber, Clement, Norris, Suffield, Glascock, Rand, Smith, Woolley, and Mundy.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-015" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-015" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Adam Verhoeve (see the previous volume, p. 191).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-016" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-016" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Left the Downs on December 13, 1629 (<em>O.C.</em> 1512). For consultations held on board see p. 362 of the last volume.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-017" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-017" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Master of the <em>Speedwell</em>, which returned to England from Bantam in 1629.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-018" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-018" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Governor-General Jan Pieterszoon Coen died in September, 1629, and was succeeded by Jacques Specx, who held the office till September, 1632.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-019" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-019" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Lansquenet, an old card game of German origin.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-020" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-020" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the previous volume, p. 202.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-021" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-021" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A variety of silk piece-goods.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-022" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-022" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. ix. no. 298.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-023" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-023" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Cf. p. 14.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-024" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-024" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the introduction to the previous volume, p. xxi.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-025" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-025" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. the King and other persons of importance (<em>umarā</em>, pl. of <em>amīr</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-026" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-026" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Persian <em>tuhfa</em>, here meaning goods suitable for presentation.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-027" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-027" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sir Francis Crane in 1619 established some tapestry works at Mortlake under the patronage of King James. The tapestries here referred to were some which he had been allowed to send out in 1629 for sale at his own risk. References to a previous consignment of a similar character will be found in the last volume.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-028" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-028" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For the seizure of the <em>Scout</em> at Aden and the imprisonment of the master (Nathaniel Best) and crew, see the introduction to the previous volume.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-029" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-029" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mr. J. J. Cotton, I.C.S., has kindly ascertained that this term means 'dead coral', and that 'tiraglia brutura' signifies the rough growths and crusts that form on the plant.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-030" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-030" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mulher (Bāglān) and Ahmadnagar.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-031" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-031" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arabic <em>qāfila</em>, a caravan. The term was also used for a fleet of light vessels carrying merchandize.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-032" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-032" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>The Embassy of Sir T. Roe</em>, pp. 96, 331, 427, etc.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-033" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-033" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Company formed, under royal auspices, to carry on Portuguese trade with the East was founded in 1630. It was a complete failure, and was extinguished by a royal decree in April, 1633.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-034" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-034" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. ix. no. 298. The Governor is there said to have replied that the trade was free to all and he could not therefore exclude the Dutch and English.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-035" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-035" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. ix. no. 298. It is there stated that the Dutch replied that they could not make any engagement without a previous reference to Batavia.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-036" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-036" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In 1628: see pp. 277, 295 of the previous volume. These disputes led to an order by the Company that no merchant returning from the Indies should assume the title of commander of the ship in which he embarked (<em>Court Minutes</em>, Feb. 16, 1631).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-037" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-037" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is in a different hand, and is signed by Skibbow, Barber, Suffield, Mountney, and Norris.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-038" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-038" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Styled later ‘John de Castro’ and ‘Mascareenhas’ (Réunion: see the last volume, p. 263).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-039" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-039" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A little later Sharp says: ‘Our Captaine [Weddell] haith a dixonarie of all ther common words; but we brought into England 3 or 4 of ther black boys, by whome the Companye may make a book mor perfect.’</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-040" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-040" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. ‘whole prows [Malay <em>prāū</em>, a boat] full.’</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-041" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-041" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The writer's name is not given, but he was evidently on board the <em>Jonas</em>. A further account, by Nicholas Prin, will be found in vol. liii of the <em>Marine Records</em>. It adds nothing of importance.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-042" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-042" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">He was the master of the <em>Discovery</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-043" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-043" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">He was a master’s mate on board the <em>William</em>. For another account, by Andrew Warden of the <em>Blessing</em>, see vol. lvi of the same series.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-044" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-044" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The small volcanic island of Trinidad in the South Atlantic, 700 miles from Brazil. For earlier visits by the Company’s ships see the volume for 1618–21, p. 271.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-045" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-045" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Of this and the other Bombay records calendared in the present volume a modern transcript will be found in the India Office series of Factory Records (<em>Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part ii).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-046" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-046" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is another copy at Bombay (<em>Surat Letters Outward</em>, vol. i. p. 15), a transcript of which will be found in the I. O. <em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. lxxxiv. part ii. p. 11.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-047" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-047" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is apparently a form of ‘capital’. It was used to denote the stock, whether of goods or money, devoted to some particular purpose.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-048" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-048" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">I am informed that these quaint terms are still in use at the Bank of England to denote good and bad debts respectively.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-049" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-049" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Malayālam <em>chāyaver</em>, a root affording a red dye much used in S. India.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-050" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-050" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Printed by Mr. G. W. Forrest, C.I.E., in his <em>Selections rom State Papers, Bombay: Home Series</em>, vol. i. p. 3.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-051" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-051" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A second copy will be found among the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-052" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-052" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Don Alvarez Botelho. The writers were of course ignorant of his death off Jambi, in the action described on a later page.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-053" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-053" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Consisting of John Skibbow, Joseph Hopkinson, Richard Barber, James Bickford, and Arthur Suffield.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-054" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-054" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sent overland by the messengers who had brought the letter to which it replies. A note states that, in consideration of the prevailing dearth, the bearers were paid 30 mahmūdīs beyond the usual allowance. This letter has been printed at full length by Mr. Forrest (<em>op. cit.</em>, p. 6).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-055" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-055" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">As will be seen later, this was incorrect.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-056" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-056" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Grapes pickled in brine—a delicacy still in favour in India.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-057" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-057" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A contemporary extract from this letter, describing the fight, is in the Public Record Office (<em>East Indies</em>, vol. iv. no. 83). The whole letter has been printed by Mr. Forrest at p. 10 of the work already mentioned.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-058" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-058" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Capitão Mór</em>, i.e. the Captain-Major, Don Francisco Coutinho.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-059" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-059" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Vāsad, where the road from Baroda to Ahmadābād crossed the Mahī.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-060" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-060" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See a note on p. 95 of the 1618–21 volume. The 'canguinees' mentioned in the next sentence were probably the same goods.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-061" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-061" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Petapoli or Peddapalli, a village three miles to the north of Nizāmpatam. The latter is on the coast, about thirty-six miles west-south-west of Masulipatam. Petapoli was once a port of considerable trade, but was left high and dry by the advance of the Kistna delta.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-062" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-062" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Motupalli, a decayed port on the coast, about twenty miles west-south-west of Nizāmpatam. Bowrey calls it ‘Montapolee’. It was in Golconda territory (<em>O.C.</em> 1486).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-063" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-063" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Vīravāsaram, a town in Bhīmavaram tāluk, Godāvarī district, eight miles north-west of Narsāpur and forty miles north-east of Masulipatam. Later on the English established a regular factory here.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-064" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-064" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>gaz:</em> see Bowrey’s <em>Countries round the Bay of Bengal</em>, p. 218.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-065" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-065" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">An obligation in writing (Ital. <em>scritto</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-066" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-066" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A letter from the Viceroy (<em>Lisbon Transcripts: Doc. Remet.</em>, bk. 29, f. 82) mentions that Coutinho is being brought to trial for his failure.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-067" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-067" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For the grant of privileges to the Dutch by the King of Golconda see <em>Corpus Diplomaticum Neerlando-Indicum</em>, by J. E. Heeres, vol. i. pp. 231–7.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-068" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-068" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Palivela, in the Amalāpuram tāluk of the Godāvarī district.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-069" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-069" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is the Arabic word for the coffee berry. Herbert, in his <em>Travels</em> (ed. 1638, p. 220), describes coffee as 'a sort of Stigian liquour, a black, thick, bitter potion, brewed out of Bunchie or Bunnu berries'.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly a <em>jubba</em>, or cotton outer garment.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For Mundy's account of their experiences see his invaluable journal (<em>Rawl. MS.</em> A 315 in the Bodleian, and <em>Harl.</em> 2286 in the British Museum).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Rao Ratan Singh Hāra, Rājā of Būndī. His services to the Mogul Emperors are well known.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">A note is added of the letters entrusted to the two factors in connexion with this business. They were addressed to the Rājā himself, to 'Hakeim Mezulzeman' [Hakīm Masīh-al-Zamān: <em>see the volume for 1622–23</em>, p. 273], to Mīrzā Jam Qulī Beg, to Is-hāq Beg, to Kāsī Dās, and to the two brokers.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>patel</em> (village head-man) Vachvohra.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a duplicate in the same volume (p. 81).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-076" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-076" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The allusion is to the sieges of 1538 and 1546.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Constantino de Sà had been defeated and slain by the King of Kandy, who had thereupon laid siege to Colombo (Faria y Sousa's <em>Asia Portuguesa</em>, vol. iii. p. 457).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Printed in part by Mr. Forrest at p. 12 of the work already quoted.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This may possibly be <em>praganā-dār</em>, ‘lord of a <em>parganā</em>.’</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Signed by Willoughby, Read, and Grove, at Masulipatam. This is a transcript (made in 1788) from the original, now lost. The official's name seems to have been Mīrzā Rōzbihān. He is mentioned as Governor of Masulipatam in the Batavia <em>Dagh-Register</em> for 1631-4, p. 45. The place has not been identified, but was probably near that town.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Printed by Mr. Forrest at p. 13 of the work already mentioned.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Port. (from Arabic) <em>almadia</em>, meaning a raft, but commonly applied also to Indian small boats or canoes. Pyrard de Laval uses it as the equivalent of the Tamil <em>tōni</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">An obscure word, probably allied to 'coign' ('quoin'). The meaning is obvious.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">'Dispeeded by the Dutch conveyance, with a bill [of] exchange [for] 30,000 rupees.' This letter has been entered twice by some mistake.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-085" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-085" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">They are entered after the next letter, but are of no general interest.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-086" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-086" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Choranda, in Baroda territory, about twenty-five miles north-east of Broach.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See Faria y Sousa's <em>Asia Portuguesa</em>, vol. iii. p. 444.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">For some correspondence between the King and the Portuguese commander see nos. 84 i-iv and 85 i-iv in vol. iv of the <em>East Indies</em> series at the Public Record Office.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pieter Vlack, Member of Council at Batavia, 1626-34.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The endorsement says : ‘ directed to Capt. Weddalls fleete ’; but this is a mistaken inference.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">An old sea term meaning ‘ready’, ‘in good order’, etc.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The same endorsement appears on other letters, but it is clear that Quail’s ship reached Plymouth in 1633.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">A copy, with marginal abstracts, forms <em>O.C.</em> 1336.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Unpolished coral brought from Sardinia, then an important centre of the industry (information from Mr. J. J. Cotton, I.C.S.).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mahmūd Ādil Shāh, King of Bījāpur.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Palm-sugar (see the previous volume, p. 236). The term was also applied to the spirit obtained from the same source; cf. the <em>Court Minutes</em> for May 20, 1631, where Weddell is said to have brought away ‘a hoggshead of jaggery for his owne drinking at sea’.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Between England and Spain (November 5, 1630).</span></li></ol></section>
